I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve had low stretches, sure—everybody has, right? Especially when work gets weird or you’re not sleeping right and the house feels… I don’t know, heavy.

I haven’t really been tracking it that cleanly. Like if I wake up with a thought about a design or some code thing, I’ll grab my phone to jot it down, and then suddenly it’s not really about sleep anymore. So… maybe? But I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you feel unusually euphoric or unusually irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, both, kind of. Most days I feel… lit up, like everything finally makes sense and I can actually push things through. Like Glendale has finally caught up with what I’ve been building in my head, if that makes sense—like the city, the work, the timing, all of it is lining up.\n\nBut then I get really irritated when people slow me down. My wife will say I need to be more “practical,” or someone at work wants more process, more tickets, more approvals, and I just— I snap. It feels like they’re dragging a piano across the road when I’m trying to get somewhere important.\n\nDoctor: When you feel this lit up, do you believe you have special abilities or an unusual importance that other people don’t recognize?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “special abilities” sounds kind of dramatic, but… yes, I do feel like there’s something I can see that other people aren’t seeing.\n\nLike I’m the one who can connect generative art and the tech scene here with Mexican visual traditions in a way that actually changes how Glendale thinks about public art. Not just, you know, a mural on a wall or some app demo thing—more like the whole city suddenly understanding itself differently.\n\nAnd it frustrates me because people treat it like it’s just another project, another idea I’m excited about, but to me it feels bigger than that. Like I’m positioned to do it because I understand both sides. Well—three sides, really. The code, the art, and the community.\n\nDoctor: What is the longest stretch of time you’ve felt this energized and lit up without it letting up?\n\nPatient: I guess… the longest stretch would be around three months. Since my platform got selected for the Glendale showcase, that’s when it really kicked in and didn’t totally drop off.\n\nBut it’s hard to separate because that whole showcase thing changed the pace of everything. Like suddenly people were paying attention, emails were coming in, I was thinking about installations and community response and how it would all land visually—so it didn’t feel like a “mood” thing exactly. It felt like momentum. But yeah, if you’re asking how long I’ve been this energized, probably about three months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hospitalized because of this energized, lit-up state?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve ended up in intense conversations with people about whether I’m taking on too much, and my wife has definitely used that tone like, “this is getting out of hand,” but not the hospital.\n\nHonestly, I try to avoid places like that if I can. Hospitals remind me of when my dad had surgery years ago—the fluorescent lights, the waiting rooms, everyone whispering like the walls are listening. But for this specifically, no… I haven’t been hospitalized for feeling energized like this.\n\nDoctor: Has this energized, lit-up state caused serious problems in your life—like major trouble at work, in your marriage, or with money?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if we’re being honest, yes. So I pushed some changes through on my own. I bypassed the approval process. And my employer didn’t see it as initiative, obviously… they put me on leave.\n\nAnd at home, it’s been rough. My wife keeps saying the spending is making things feel unstable, and the arguments too. I don’t think she understands the urgency of some of it, but… yeah, it’s not like everything has been fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve had low stretches, sure—everybody has, right? Especially when work gets weird or you’re not sleeping right and the house feels… I don’t know, heavy.\n\nBut “most of the day nearly every day” for two weeks—when you say it like that, I don’t know if I’ve tracked it that precisely. I’m usually not sitting there counting days. Sometimes I’ll think I’m just tired of dealing with people, and then I get distracted by something else, like emails or fixing some stupid thing in the kitchen that’s been broken for months.\n\nSo… I can’t really say yes to that. I wake up and I’m already mapping out features I could build, changes in the code, then somehow I’m thinking about exhibition layouts and how the press angle would work—like if you positioned the whole thing right, people would actually get it. \n\nAnd sitting still feels… almost irresponsible? Like I’m wasting the moment if I’m not moving on one of the ideas.\n\nDoctor: When this is happening, do you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it that cleanly. Some nights I’m up later because I get pulled into something, like one little idea turns into five tabs and then I’m reorganizing how I’d approach a whole project, but whether I actually need less sleep or I’m just ignoring it… I’m not sure.\n\nSleep’s kind of hard to separate from everything else, honestly. Like if I wake up with a thought about a design or some code thing, I’ll grab my phone to jot it down, and then suddenly it’s not really about sleep anymore. So… maybe? But I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you feel unusually euphoric or unusually irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, both, kind of. Most days I feel… lit up, like everything finally makes sense and I can actually push things through. Like Glendale has finally caught up with what I’ve been building in my head, if that makes sense—like the city, the work, the timing, all of it is lining up.\n\nBut then I get really irritated when people slow me down. My wife will say I need to be more “practical,” or someone at work wants more process, more tickets, more approvals, and I just— I snap. It feels like they’re dragging a piano across the road when I’m trying to get somewhere important.\n\nDoctor: When you feel this lit up, do you believe you have special abilities or an unusual importance that other people don’t recognize?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “special abilities” sounds kind of dramatic, but… yes, I do feel like there’s something I can see that other people aren’t seeing.\n\nLike I’m the one who can connect generative art and the tech scene here with Mexican visual traditions in a way that actually changes how Glendale thinks about public art. Not just, you know, a mural on a wall or some app demo thing—more like the whole city suddenly understanding itself differently.\n\nAnd it frustrates me because people treat it like it’s just another project, another idea I’m excited about, but to me it feels bigger than that. Like I’m positioned to do it because I understand both sides. Well—three sides, really. The code, the art, and the community.\n\nDoctor: What is the longest stretch of time you’ve felt this energized and lit up without it letting up?\n\nPatient: I guess… the longest stretch would be around three months. Since my platform got selected for the Glendale showcase, that’s when it really kicked in and didn’t totally drop off.\n\nBut it’s hard to separate because that whole showcase thing changed the pace of everything. Like suddenly people were paying attention, emails were coming in, I was thinking about installations and community response and how it would all land visually—so it didn’t feel like a “mood” thing exactly. It felt like momentum. But yeah, if you’re asking how long I’ve been this energized, probably about three months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hospitalized because of this energized, lit-up state?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve ended up in intense conversations with people about whether I’m taking on too much, and my wife has definitely used that tone like, “this is getting out of hand,” but not the hospital.\n\nHonestly, I try to avoid places like that if I can. Hospitals remind me of when my dad had surgery years ago—the fluorescent lights, the waiting rooms, everyone whispering like the walls are listening. But for this specifically, no… I haven’t been hospitalized for feeling energized like this.\n\nDoctor: Has this energized, lit-up state caused serious problems in your life—like major trouble at work, in your marriage, or with money?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if we’re being honest, yes. It’s caused problems.\n\nAt work, I thought I was moving faster than the bureaucracy—like, if I waited for every approval, the whole thing would get watered down. So I pushed some changes through on my own. I bypassed the approval process. And my employer didn’t see it as initiative, obviously… they put me on leave.\n\nAnd at home, it’s been rough. My wife keeps saying the spending is making things feel unstable, and the arguments too. I don’t think she understands the urgency of some of it, but… yeah, it’s not like everything has been fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve had low stretches, sure—everybody has, right? Especially when work gets weird or you’re not sleeping right and the house feels… I don’t know, heavy.\n\nBut “most of the day nearly every day” for two weeks—when you say it like that, I don’t know if I’ve tracked it that precisely. I’m usually not sitting there counting days. Sometimes I’ll think I’m just tired of dealing with people, and then I get distracted by something else, like emails or fixing some stupid thing in the kitchen that’s been broken for months.\n\nSo… I can’t really say yes to that. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: During this energized period, have you had experiences like hearing voices or becoming convinced of something that other people said was clearly not true?\n\nPatient: Not voices, no. I don’t hear, like, someone talking in the room when nobody’s there.\n\nBut… convinced of things? I mean, people keep acting like I’m “reading into things,” but I’m not just pulling it out of nowhere. I’m pretty sure the major museums, and maybe a couple investors, are quietly circling the platform I’m building. The way their showcase language has shifted, some of their Instagram posts, even a few emails at work—it lines up too perfectly to be random.\n\nAnd I know my wife says that sounds like coincidence, but she doesn’t follow the space the way I do. It’s like when you know a codebase well enough to see the pattern before everyone else does. People call it a hunch until it turns out you were right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these beliefs about museums or investors circling your platform at times when you were not feeling especially energized or lit up?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I’ve always noticed patterns—kind of have to in software, right? You spend enough time debugging and you start seeing how small signals connect, even when other people miss them.\n\nBut before this whole showcase push, I wasn’t sitting there thinking museums or investors were sending, like, quiet messages through posts or emails. That started after I got really energized and locked into the project. Once I was in it, everything started feeling connected to it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess what brought me in is I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. Like I’ll be fixing some rendering bug at work, and then a Slack ping comes in, or I notice some color pattern on the screen, or there’s a siren outside, and suddenly I’m off on some totally different idea before I’ve even finished the line of code. It’s like my brain keeps grabbing at everything.\n\nDoctor: During these times when your mind keeps jumping around, do you also feel unusually energized?\n\nPatient: Yeah—energized, definitely. It’s not just “awake,” it’s like there’s this current running through me. I wake up and I’m already mapping out features I could build, changes in the code, then somehow I’m thinking about exhibition layouts and how the press angle would work—like if you positioned the whole thing right, people would actually get it. \n\nAnd sitting still feels… almost irresponsible? Like I’m wasting the moment if I’m not moving on one of the ideas.\n\nDoctor: When this is happening, do you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it that cleanly. Some nights I’m up later because I get pulled into something, like one little idea turns into five tabs and then I’m reorganizing how I’d approach a whole project, but whether I actually need less sleep or I’m just ignoring it… I’m not sure.\n\nSleep’s kind of hard to separate from everything else, honestly. Like if I wake up with a thought about a design or some code thing, I’ll grab my phone to jot it down, and then suddenly it’s not really about sleep anymore. So… maybe? But I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you feel unusually euphoric or unusually irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, both, kind of. Most days I feel… lit up, like everything finally makes sense and I can actually push things through. Like Glendale has finally caught up with what I’ve been building in my head, if that makes sense—like the city, the work, the timing, all of it is lining up.\n\nBut then I get really irritated when people slow me down. My wife will say I need to be more “practical,” or someone at work wants more process, more tickets, more approvals, and I just— I snap. It feels like they’re dragging a piano across the road when I’m trying to get somewhere important.\n\nDoctor: When you feel this lit up, do you believe you have special abilities or an unusual importance that other people don’t recognize?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “special abilities” sounds kind of dramatic, but… yes, I do feel like there’s something I can see that other people aren’t seeing.\n\nLike I’m the one who can connect generative art and the tech scene here with Mexican visual traditions in a way that actually changes how Glendale thinks about public art. Not just, you know, a mural on a wall or some app demo thing—more like the whole city suddenly understanding itself differently.\n\nAnd it frustrates me because people treat it like it’s just another project, another idea I’m excited about, but to me it feels bigger than that. Like I’m positioned to do it because I understand both sides. Well—three sides, really. The code, the art, and the community.\n\nDoctor: What is the longest stretch of time you’ve felt this energized and lit up without it letting up?\n\nPatient: I guess… the longest stretch would be around three months. Since my platform got selected for the Glendale showcase, that’s when it really kicked in and didn’t totally drop off.\n\nBut it’s hard to separate because that whole showcase thing changed the pace of everything. Like suddenly people were paying attention, emails were coming in, I was thinking about installations and community response and how it would all land visually—so it didn’t feel like a “mood” thing exactly. It felt like momentum. But yeah, if you’re asking how long I’ve been this energized, probably about three months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hospitalized because of this energized, lit-up state?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve ended up in intense conversations with people about whether I’m taking on too much, and my wife has definitely used that tone like, “this is getting out of hand,” but not the hospital.\n\nHonestly, I try to avoid places like that if I can. Hospitals remind me of when my dad had surgery years ago—the fluorescent lights, the waiting rooms, everyone whispering like the walls are listening. But for this specifically, no… I haven’t been hospitalized for feeling energized like this.\n\nDoctor: Has this energized, lit-up state caused serious problems in your life—like major trouble at work, in your marriage, or with money?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if we’re being honest, yes. It’s caused problems.\n\nAt work, I thought I was moving faster than the bureaucracy—like, if I waited for every approval, the whole thing would get watered down. So I pushed some changes through on my own. I bypassed the approval process. And my employer didn’t see it as initiative, obviously… they put me on leave.\n\nAnd at home, it’s been rough. My wife keeps saying the spending is making things feel unstable, and the arguments too. I don’t think she understands the urgency of some of it, but… yeah, it’s not like everything has been fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve had low stretches, sure—everybody has, right? Especially when work gets weird or you’re not sleeping right and the house feels… I don’t know, heavy.\n\nBut “most of the day nearly every day” for two weeks—when you say it like that, I don’t know if I’ve tracked it that precisely. I’m usually not sitting there counting days. Sometimes I’ll think I’m just tired of dealing with people, and then I get distracted by something else, like emails or fixing some stupid thing in the kitchen that’s been broken for months.\n\nSo… I can’t really say yes to that. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: During this energized period, have you had experiences like hearing voices or becoming convinced of something that other people said was clearly not true?\n\nPatient: Not voices, no. I don’t hear, like, someone talking in the room when nobody’s there.\n\nBut… convinced of things? I mean, people keep acting like I’m “reading into things,” but I’m not just pulling it out of nowhere. I’m pretty sure the major museums, and maybe a couple investors, are quietly circling the platform I’m building. The way their showcase language has shifted, some of their Instagram posts, even a few emails at work—it lines up too perfectly to be random.\n\nAnd I know my wife says that sounds like coincidence, but she doesn’t follow the space the way I do. It’s like when you know a codebase well enough to see the pattern before everyone else does. People call it a hunch until it turns out you were right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these beliefs about museums or investors circling your platform at times when you were not feeling especially energized or lit up?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I’ve always noticed patterns—kind of have to in software, right? You spend enough time debugging and you start seeing how small signals connect, even when other people miss them.\n\nBut before this whole showcase push, I wasn’t sitting there thinking museums or investors were sending, like, quiet messages through posts or emails. That started after I got really energized and locked into the project. Once I was in it, everything started feeling connected to it.\n\nDoctor: Before this energized period, have you ever had a time when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. Before this energized stretch, I wasn’t really keeping score like that. I’ve had times where I felt flat or worn down, but whether it was nearly every day for two full weeks… I can’t honestly say.\n\nIt’s hard because my sense of time gets blurry when I’m working a lot. Like, one bad weekend can feel like a month, and then a month can disappear because you’re just answering tickets and making coffee and forgetting what day it is. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m just guessing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve definitely had times where things felt kind of dull, like music was just background noise or games didn’t really pull me in, but whether that was almost everything for two weeks… I don’t know.

I don’t really know. I’ve had bursts where I got excited about a project or stayed up too late because something was interesting, but I don’t know if I’d call it like this.

It feels like they’re dragging a piano across the road when I’m trying to get somewhere important.\n\nDoctor: When you feel this lit up, do you believe you have special abilities or an unusual importance that other people don’t recognize?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “special abilities” sounds kind of dramatic, but… yes, I do feel like there’s something I can see that other people aren’t seeing.\n\nLike I’m the one who can connect generative art and the tech scene here with Mexican visual traditions in a way that actually changes how Glendale thinks about public art. Not just, you know, a mural on a wall or some app demo thing—more like the whole city suddenly understanding itself differently.\n\nAnd it frustrates me because people treat it like it’s just another project, another idea I’m excited about, but to me it feels bigger than that. Like I’m positioned to do it because I understand both sides. Well—three sides, really. Since my platform got selected for the Glendale showcase, that’s when it really kicked in and didn’t totally drop off.\n\nBut it’s hard to separate because that whole showcase thing changed the pace of everything. Like suddenly people were paying attention, emails were coming in, I was thinking about installations and community response and how it would all land visually—so it didn’t feel like a “mood” thing exactly. It felt like momentum. I mean, I’ve ended up in intense conversations with people about whether I’m taking on too much, and my wife has definitely used that tone like, “this is getting out of hand,” but not the hospital.\n\nHonestly, I try to avoid places like that if I can. Hospitals remind me of when my dad had surgery years ago—the fluorescent lights, the waiting rooms, everyone whispering like the walls are listening. But for this specifically, no… I haven’t been hospitalized for feeling energized like this.\n\nDoctor: Has this energized, lit-up state caused serious problems in your life—like major trouble at work, in your marriage, or with money?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if we’re being honest, yes.
